[BODY] Now not only the antithesis is here complete, but the <sty c="000000" i="1">order</sty> also in which it occurs coresponds with what is often the style of the Prophets; the first part of the first clause corresponds with the last part of the second, and the last of the former with the first of the latter; and not according to Dr. <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, who represents the clauses as regularly antithetic. See a similar instance in chapter 1:13, and also in the first verse of this chapter. The man who faints, and he who lives by faith, form the contrast; and the addition “by faith” in the latter clause implies the fainting to be through want of faith, or through unbelief. Then the soul that is not right stands in contrast with the righteous, or the just in the second line. Thus every thing in the verse itself, and in its connection with what precedes it, is in favor of what has been proposed. And <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>seemed disposed to adopt this reading. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

--- PAGE 165 ---

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft26</sty> Though the general meaning of the beginning of this verse is what most
[BODY] critics agree in, yet the construction is difficult. The only difference as to the meaning is, whether the proud man is said to be given to wine, or is compared to such an one, or to wine itself. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> takes the first, and gives this version —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Moreover, as a mighty man transgresseth through wine,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">He is proud, and remaineth not at rest.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, agreeing with <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Mede</sty>, takes the latter sense, and renders the line as follows: —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Moreoever wine is treacherous;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The haughty man stayeth not at home.</sty>

[BODY] This is rather a paraphrase than a version; but this is the meaning of which the words are most capable. The two first participles need not be connected according to what <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin </sty>proposes. Then the distich may be thus rendered —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And truly, as wine is treacherous,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">So</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> is the proud man, and he will not rest.</sty>

[INDENT] Then follows a delineation of his character —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Because he enlarges as the grave his desire,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And he is like death and cannot be satisfied;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For he gathers to himself all the nations,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And collects to himself all the people.</sty>

[BODY] As to wine being treacherous, see <sty c="800000" i="0"><203001></sty>Proverbs 30:1. Wine is pleasant to the taste and inviting in its color, but degrading, when taken immoderately, in its effects; so a proud and arrogant man is at first glittering and plausible, and splendid in his appearance, but afterwards cruel and oppressive. This seems to be the most obvious similitude, as contained in the passage.
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst </sty>renders the two first lines as follows —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Yea, </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">as </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">when wine deceiveth a man,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">So </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">he is proud, and is not at rest.</sty>

[BODY] He interprets “proud,” as meaning “intoxicated with power and dominion,” and refers to <sty c="800000" i="0"><270430></sty>Daniel 4:30. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft27</sty> This can hardly be allowed; for in this case the final letter of the
[BODY] previous word must have been [<sty c="008080" i="0">t</sty>] and not [<sty c="008080" i="0">h</sty>]. It is a word evidently in appostion, designing the character of the proverb and the taunt, they

--- PAGE 166 ---

[CENTERED] being enigmas, conveyed in a highly figurative language. The whole verse may be thus rendered —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Shall not these, all of them,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Raise against him a proverb and a taunt —</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Enigmas for him;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Yea, say will </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">every one</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> —</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">“Woe to him who multiplies </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">what </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">is not his own! how long!</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">“And to him who accumulates on himself thick clay!”</sty>

[BODY] To render the last word [<sty c="008080" i="0">fyfb[</sty>], (or [<sty c="008080" i="0">fyf b[</sty>], apart, as given by ten MSS.,) “pledges,” as it is done by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, does not comport at all with the rest of the passage. The Septuagint favor the common explanation, and also the <sty c="000000" i="1">Vulgate</sty>, and most commentators. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft28</sty> It is rendered impersonally by <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome </sty>“et dicetur — and it shall be
[BODY] said.” <sty c="000000" i="1">Junius </sty>introduces a question, and supposed the just, who lives by faith to be referred to — “And shall not he, <sty c="000000" i="1">i.e., </sty>the just, say?” But <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius </sty>considers that God is the speaker — “And he, <sty c="000000" i="1">i.e., </sty>God, shall say.” But the most obvious construction is, that each one of the nations previously mentioned is introduced as speaking — “Unusquisque illorum — every one of them,” is understood, says <sty c="000000" i="1">Piscator</sty>. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft29</sty> This is rendered by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, “that have lent thee on usury;” but
[BODY] incorrectly, as the corresponding clause is found in the following, and not, as he ays, in the preceding line. The literal version is as follows, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Shall not suddenly arise thy biters,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And awake thy tormentors,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And thou become for spoils to them?</sty>

[BODY] Now, the two corresponding words are “biters” and “tormentors;” and the idea of lending on usury cannot be admitted; and the common meaning of the word [<sty c="008080" i="0">˚çn</sty>], is to bite, and means lending on usury only in Hiphil. What the Septuagint gives is <sty c="0000d4" i="0">daknontev</sty> — biters.
[BODY] Here is an instance of the peculiar manner of the Prophets, and also of the writers of the New Testament; the most obvious act is mentioned first “arise,” and then what is previous to it, “awake.” There is also a similar difference in “biters” and “tormentors,” or those who vex and harass: to torment or vex is not so great an evil as to bite, as it were, like a serpent; for such is the biting meant here. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

--- PAGE 167 ---

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft30</sty> So <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> regard the passage: the land, and
[BODY] the city, are supposed to have been used poetically for lands and cities. The word rendered “violence,” [<sty c="008080" i="0">smj</sty>], means an unjust or wrong act done by force, an outrage, a violent injustice: hence <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty> rightly renders it here, “direptionem — robbing, pillaging, or plundering.” While <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>and others apply the passage to Judea and Jerusalem, the Septuagint version would lead us to suppose that Babylon was intended. The view taken here would be the most probable, were it not that the words are repeated at the end of verse 17; and there clearly they refer to the land of Judea and Jerusalem. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft31</sty> Literally, “sinning thy soul.” We have in <sty c="800000" i="0"><200836></sty>Proverbs 8:36, [<sty c="008080" i="0">yafh</sty>],
[BODY] “my sinner,” rendered no doubt correctly, “he that sinneth against me.” So here “sinning thy soul,” means “sinning against thy soul.” See the same words in <sty c="800000" i="0"><202002></sty>Proverbs 20:2. In <sty c="800000" i="0"><041638></sty>Numbers 16:38, the preposition [<sty c="008080" i="0">b</sty>] is before “souls.” “Thy soul hath sinned,” as given by the Septuagint, and adopted by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, does not convey the meaning; for to sin against our souls, is to injure ourselves so as to bring down judgment, as in the case mentioned in <sty c="800000" i="0"><041638></sty>Numbers 16:38, while the other phrase conveys only the idea of doing what is wrong. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft32</sty> The word rendered here “Wood,” <sty c="000000" i="1">lignum</sty>, is [<sty c="008080" i="0">sypk</sty>], and only found
[BODY] here. The Septuagint has <sty c="0000d4" i="0">kanqarov</sty>, a beetle, — <sty c="000000" i="1">Sym.</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">sundesmov</sty>, bond, tie, or joint, — <sty c="000000" i="1">Theod.</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">e]ndesmov</sty>, bandage or jointing. The context shows that it must be something connected with woodbuilding. <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst </sty>says, that it is a verb in Syriac, and means to connect, to fasten together, and he renders it a beam or a rafter, which would exactly suit this place. The word, [<sty c="008080" i="0">≈[m</sty>], “from the wood,” evidently means the wood-building or wood-work. So that <sty c="000000" i="1">tabulatum</sty>, a story or a chamber in a building, as rendered by <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>, is not amiss. Perhaps the best version would be, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And the beam from the wood-work answers it.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Bochart </sty>says, that [<sty c="008080" i="0">sypk</sty>], in Rabbinical writings, means a brick, and that it was usual formerly, as it was in this country not long ago, to build with bricks and wood or timber together; and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson </sty>has adopted this meaning, but the other is more satisfactory. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

--- PAGE 168 ---

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft33</sty> The construction of the first line of this verse, as given by <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>, is
[BODY] stiff and unnatural. There is no doubt but that [<sty c="008080" i="0">hnh</sty>] is a pronoun in the plural number, and so it has been taken by the Septuagint, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">tauta</sty>, these things, and such is the rendering of the Syriac and Arabic versions. No improvement, perhaps, can be made on <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome’s</sty> rendering of this verse, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Are not these things from Jehovah </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">God </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">of hosts,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">That people should labor for the fire,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And nations should weary themselves for a vain thing?</sty>

[BODY] The intimation is, that all the buildings erected by blood and prepared by iniquity, were destined for the fire. “For the fire,” [<sty c="008080" i="0">ça ydb</sty>], literally is, for the supply of fire, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst </sty>renders the phrase: then it is, for the supply of emptiness or vacuity, [<sty c="008080" i="0">qyr yrb</sty>].
[BODY] The last two lines, with some variety, are found in <sty c="800000" i="0"><245158></sty>Jeremiah 51:58, and applied to Babylon. In Jeremiah, “for a vain thing,” is in the first line, and “for the fire” is in the second. Jeremiah puts the less evil first, and the greatest last; but Habakkuk’s usual manner is the reverse, which has been before noticed, and we find an instance in the preceding verse, where he mentions “blood” first, and in the next line, “iniquity.”
[CENTERED] That the destination of Babylon for the fire is here meant, seems evident from the following verse. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><245125></sty>Jeremiah 51:25. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft34</sty> The idea is nearly the same, though not the words. The verse in Isaiah
[BODY] is literally this —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">For fill the earth shall the knowledge of Jehovah,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Like the waters spreading over the sea.</sty>

[BODY] The verb rendered “cover” here and in Isaiah is, [<sty c="008080" i="0">hsk</sty>], which means first to spread, and in the second place to cover, as the effect of spreading. It is followed here by [<sty c="008080" i="0">l[</sty>], over, and by [<sty c="008080" i="0">l</sty>], over, in Isaiah; and so spreading must be the idea included in the verb. The comparison in Isaiah is between knowledge and waters, and the earth and the sea. Hence the common version does not properly present the comparison. The verb [<sty c="008080" i="0">alm</sty>], is used in a passive and active sense. See
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><010613></sty>Genesis 6:13, and <sty c="800000" i="0"><010122></sty>Genesis 1:22; 24:16. This verse may
[BODY] be rendered in <sty c="000000" i="1">Welsh </sty>word for word, without changing the order in one instance: —

--- PAGE 169 ---

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Canys henwa y ddaear wybodaeth o Jehova,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Vel y dyvroedd dros y more yn ymdaenu.</sty>

[BODY] “The knowledge of Jeohovah,” [<sty c="008080" i="0">hwhyAta h[d</sty>], is not an instance of a genitive case by juxtaposition, which is common both in Hebrew and in Welsh; for [<sty c="008080" i="0">ta</sty>] here must be a preposition, “from,” for it is sometimes used for [<sty c="008080" i="0">tam</sty>]. It is a knowledge that was to come from Jehovah, and not a knowledge of Jehovah. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft35</sty> There is no reason to doubt but that this is the meaning of the sentence
[BODY] here: and it is a striking instance of the variety of meaning which belongs to similar expressions, when differently connected. The glory of God is manifested by judgments as well as by mercies. In Isaiah it is “the knowledge of or from Jehovah;” here the expression is, “the knowledge of the glory of Jehovah.” By “the knowledge of Jehovah” is to be understood the revelation made by the gospel. But by “the knowledge of his glory” is meant evidently the display of his power in destroying Babylon, as power is often signified by glory. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft36</sty> The rendering of this verse has been various, though mos tagree as to its
[BODY] import. <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, take nearly the same view of its meaning as <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>, regarding it as metaphorical. But <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty> thinks that the drunkenness which the king of Babylon produced, means the evils which he inflicted on other nations. To make a nation drunk was to subdue and oppress it. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><235117></sty>Isaiah 51:17,22;
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><242515></sty>Jeremiah 25:15,16, 27,28; 51:7,39,57. This view is confirmed
[BODY] by the following verse, where the king of Babylon is threatened with a similar judgment; he was also to drink of the cup of Jehovah’s right hand. As he made other nations drunk, so the Lord threatens him with a like visitation.
[CENTERED] The verse will admit of a much simpler rendering than what has been commonly offered, such as the following: —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Woe to him who makes his neighbor to drink,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Who adds his bottle, and also strong drink,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">In order that he may look on their nakedness.</sty>

[BODY] To render [<sty c="008080" i="0">hmj</sty>], wrath, or heat, or gall, or poison, as some have done, is to introduce an idea foreign to the context, and the word is often found to signify the bottle of skin in which wine was kept. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> renders it “flagon.” By mentioning bottle, abundance of wine was

--- PAGE 170 ---

[BODY] probably intended, and to this abundance was added the strong drink, [<sty c="008080" i="0">rkç</sty>], intoxicating liquor. It is commonly rendered as though it were a verb in Hiphil; but it is not so. It means here no doubt, as in other places, strong drink. This line is only an application, as we find often in the Prophets, of the preceding line.
[BODY] Though there is no MS. which has “his” instead of “thy” connected with “bottle,” yet the preceding and the following lines seem to require it; and this is the reading of <sty c="000000" i="1">Symmachus </sty>and of the <sty c="000000" i="1">Vulgate</sty>. The change of persons, it is true, is very common in the Prophets, but not in such a way as we find here, the third person being adopted both in the preceding and in the following line.
[BODY] The idea of drinking as a judgment may have arisen from the cup of malediction given to criminals before their execution. See also Psalms 75:8. Babylon is in <sty c="800000" i="0"><245107></sty>Jeremiah 51:7, represented as “a golden cup” in God’s hand to make the nations drunken. It was “golden” to signify an outward appearance that was plausible, and alluring. So the mystic Babylon is said to have a golden cup, which was full of all abominations, <sty c="800000" i="0"><661704></sty>Revelation 17:4. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft37</sty> The view presented here of the first clause of the verse is striking, and
[BODY] such as the words may admit. But most commentators attach to them another meaning. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome’s</sty> version is —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Thou art filled with shame instead of glory.</sty>

[INDENT] <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson’s </sty>rendering is —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Thou art filled with shame, not with glory.</sty>

[BODY] The verb being in the past tense seems to favor <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin’s </sty>view — “Thou hast been satiated with shame from glory,” that is, thou hast been filled to satiety with the shame occasioned to others, arising from the pursuit of thine own glory. And then, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> justly observes, his punishment is denounced. — “Drink thou also.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft38</sty> The verb [<sty c="008080" i="0">bwst</sty>], loosely expressed here, is very correctly rendered by
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> “shall come round;” and this is the idea which <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> suggests in the following explanation. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft39</sty> It is commonly derived from [<sty c="008080" i="0">yq</sty>], a contraction of [<sty c="008080" i="0">ayq</sty>], a vomit or
[BODY] spewing, and [<sty c="008080" i="0">zwlq</sty>], shame. Compounds are no common things in Hebrew; and these are found separate in nine MSS. The <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>

--- PAGE 171 ---

[BODY] have <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ajtimia</sty>, reproach only; and the <sty c="000000" i="1">Vulgate</sty>, “vomitus ignominiae — the spewing of shame.” <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> renders it “foul shame,” and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> “great ignominy,” regarding it as a reduplicate noun for [<sty c="008080" i="0">zwlqlq</sty>]. But as drunkenness is the metaphor used, “shameful spewing,” or the spewing of shame or of reproach is most suitable to the passage. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft40</sty> It is commonly agreed, that Libanus here means either the temple or the
[BODY] land of Judah; most probably the last, according to the opinion of <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, and others. The “violence,” or outrage, of Libanus, means the violence <sty c="000000" i="1">done </sty>to it, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>and others render the clause. The next line is more difficult: if the verb be retained as it is, we must either adopt what <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> has proposed, and after him <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, or take the [<sty c="008080" i="0">w</sty>] at the beginning as a particle of comparison, according to what is done by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, “As the destruction of beasts terrifieth them.” But to preserve the parallelism of the two lines, it would be better to adopt the correction of all the early versions, <sty c="000000" i="1">Sept. Arab. Syr.</sty> and also of the <sty c="000000" i="1">Chald.</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">par.;</sty> which substitute [<sty c="008080" i="0">d</sty>] for [<sty c="008080" i="0">z</sty>] and make the verb to be [<sty c="008080" i="0">dtyjy</sty>]: and there are two MSS. which have [<sty c="008080" i="0">tjy</sty>]. In this case the rendering would be the following —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Because the violence done to Libanus shall overwhelm thee;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And the depredation done to the beasts shall rend thee;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">On account of the blood of men, and of violence to the land,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">To the city, and to all who dwelt in it.</sty>

[BODY] The reason men are called “beasts” is because Libanus is mentioned which was inhabited by beasts; and in the two following lines the statement is more clear, and according to the order usually observed, “the depredation done to beasts” is “the blood of men;” and “the violence to Libanus” is “violence to the land.” And then, as it is often the ase in the Prophets, there is an addition made to the two last lines, “To the city,” etc. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft41</sty> Rightly to understand this verse, it is necessary to remember that the
[BODY] graven and the molten image was the same; it was first graven and then covered with some metal, either of gold or of silver. See Note on
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><330101></sty>Micah 1:7, volume 3, p. 167.
[BODY] This verse, as given in our version and in that of <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, presents hardly a meaning; and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> is not justified in the peculiar sense

--- PAGE 172 ---

[CENTERED] he gives to the particle [<sty c="008080" i="0">yk</sty>], taking it as a relative pronoun. The rendering of <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin </sty>gives an evident and a striking sense. The verse may be thus literally rendered —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">18.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> What avails the graven image? —</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For its graver has formed it, —</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The molten image and the teacher of falsehood?</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For trust in it does the former of its form,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">After having made dumb idols.</sty>

[BODY] The last line show that the singular number before used is to be taken in a collective sense: and the preposition [<sty c="008080" i="0">l</sty>] before an infinitive has sometimes the meaning of “after.” See <sty c="800000" i="0"><021901></sty>Exodus 19:1, “When he has made,” etc., is the rendering of <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft42</sty> With the exception of the clause, “It will teach,” there is a general
[BODY] agreement in the mode of rendering this verse. “Shall it teach,” is <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome’s</sty> version. <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> considers it to be ironical, “It teach!” <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty> agrees with <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>, “It will itself teach thee,” that is, that it is deaf, and no god. I regard the verse as capable of a simpler and more literal rendering, as follows:
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">19.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> Woe to him who saith to the wood, “Awake, Arise;”</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">To the dumb stone, “It will teach:”</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Behold, it is covered with gold and silver!</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Yet there is no breath within it.</sty>

[CENTERED] The two verbs, “Awake, Arise,” stand connected with “wood,” and they are so given in the Septuagint; and there is a striking contrast between the dumb stone and teaching. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 3</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft43</sty> The more correct rendering here would be, “A Prayer (or rather, An
[BODY] Intercession) by Habakkuk the Prophet;” that is, It was a prayer composed by him. The preposition [<sty c="008080" i="0">l</sty>] before Habakkuk, as often before David in the Psalms, would be better rendered in this way, than by “of;” for the meaning is, not that it was his prayer, that is, one offered up by him, but that it was composed by him. “A Psalm of David,” ought to be, “A Psalm by David.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft44</sty> This explanation, adopted by <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>, is derived originally from <sty c="000000" i="1">Aquila</sty>
[BODY] and <sty c="000000" i="1">Symmachus</sty>, who rendered the phrase, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ejpi ajgohmatwn</sty>, —

--- PAGE 173 ---

[BODY] respecting oversights or errors: and they have been followed by <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Vulgate</sty>, etc. The prior version of the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty> is, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">met j</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">wjddhv</sty>, — with an ode. that this prayer is composed in metre, is evident from the word, “Selah,” and from the conclusion of the chapter. The most probable meaning of the word is what <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty> has suggested, and adopted by <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson,</sty> and that is, that it refers to a peculiar metre, a kind of composition, which from its irregularity is called <sty c="000000" i="1">erratica cantio</sty>, an erratic verse. “The prayer of Habakkuk,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, “was to be sung according to the odes which they called <sty c="000000" i="1">Sigionoth</sty>.” To the same purpose is what <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty> says, that is, it is “a song according to the notes of an ancient ode which began with this word.” It is derived from [<sty c="008080" i="0">hgç</sty>], to go astray, to wander, that is, in this instance, from the regular metre of an ode. It is an erratic ode, that is, one containing varieties. It may be thus paraphrastically expressed, “According to the notes of the irregular ode;” or, as it is in the margin of our Bibles, “According to variable songs or tunes.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft45</sty> The verb, “territus sum, — I feared,” has been omitted. It is even
[BODY] omitted in the French version. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft46</sty> The view given of “the middle of the years,” is ingenious and striking;
[BODY] but the common interpretation is, that “the years” of calamity, allotted to the Jews, are meant. The Septuagint version of this verse is so extremely wide of the original, that none can account for the differences. There are no various readings of any moment; and the literal rendering of this verse, and of the former part of the following, I consider to be this, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">2.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> O Jehovah! I have heard thy report;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">I feared, O Jehovah!</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Thy work! in the midst of the years revive it;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">In the midst of the years make it known;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">In anger remember mercy:</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">3.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> May God from Teman come</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And the Holy One from mount Paran. Selah.</sty>

[BODY] It is called “thy report,” as it was a report which came from God; the allusion is to the threatenings in chapter 1. “The report from thee,” would convey the sense. The third line is a prayer; and so are the following lines, though all the verbs are in the future tense, while that

--- PAGE 174 ---

[BODY] for “revive” is in the imperative mood. The third verse ought to end with the word “Selah.” What follows in the other part and in the subsequent verses, is a relation of what took place when God had formerly interfered in behalf of Israel; while here, and in the latter part of the preceding verse, the Prophet expresses a prayer to God in reference to his people, and borrows his language from the past interpositions of God. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft47</sty> The word [<sty c="008080" i="0">hls</sty>] is found 70 times, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst</sty> says, in the Psalms,
[BODY] and thrice in this chapter. “It was most probably,” he adds, “a <sty c="000000" i="1">note of</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">music,</sty> or a direction to the singers in the temple service to <sty c="000000" i="1">raise</sty> their voices or instruments where it is inserted.” The opinion of <sty c="000000" i="1">Gesenius</sty> is the same, it being a direction, as he says, “to repeat the preceding verse in a <sty c="000000" i="1">louder</sty> strain.” It is always rendered by the Septuagint <sty c="0000d4" i="0">Diayalma</sty>, which means a variation in singing.
[BODY] Some have rendered the word pause, but it cannot be so considered, for it occurs at the <sty c="000000" i="1">end</sty> of at least three of the Psalms. There seems to be no regularity in its adoption. in some of the Psalms it occurs once, in some twice, in others thrice, and in one psalm four times.
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> has not referred, in his comment, to the latter part of this verse, which, according to his Latin, may be thus translated, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Cover the heavens did his glory;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">With his praise full was the earth.</sty>

[BODY] Both glory and praise here are to be taken as signifying their manifestations. The reference is made to the displays of divine majesty on mount Sinai. The original may be thus rendered —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Cover the heavens did his shining,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And his lustre filled the earth. — </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Ed.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft48</sty> That [<sty c="008080" i="0">zrq</sty>] means to irradiate or to shine, is claer from <sty c="800000" i="0"><023429></sty>Exodus
[BODY] 34:29,30,35; “for shine did the skin of his face,” [<sty c="008080" i="0">yynp rw[ zrq yk</sty>]. Most critics consider that the noun here, though in this sense in no other instance, means rays, or beams of light; and this corresponds with the description given elsewhere of God’s appearance on mount Sinai. <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius, Marckius, Newcome,</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, render it “rays.” The line then would literally be —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Rays from his hand </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">were </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">to him.</sty>

--- PAGE 175 ---

[INDENT] <sty c="000000" i="1">or</sty>, to retain the English idiom.
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">He had rays from his hand.</sty>

[BODY] To render the line, “Rays streamed from his hand,” is to give a paraphrase.
[BODY] The objection of <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> as to the next line, seems not valid; for the hiding of strength may refer to the hand, or to the place, Sinai, whether we render the previous word, rays or horns; — to the place, if we retain our present reading, [<sty c="008080" i="0">hz[</sty>], “of its strength;” but to the hand, if we adopt the reading of <sty c="000000" i="1">many </sty>copies, [<sty c="008080" i="0">wz[</sty>] “of his strength,” which is perhaps the most accordant with the passage. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft49</sty> Most agree in the view given of this verse, only there is some shade of
[BODY] difference as tot he word [<sty c="008080" i="0">ãçr</sty>]; but though <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> renders it <sty c="000000" i="1">carbo</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">ignitus</sty> — ignited coals, yet in his exposition he seems to regard it with many others as a burning disease. In the six other instances in which the word occurs, it certainly has not this sense, except it be in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><053224></sty>Deuteronomy 32:24, which is doubtful. It signifies not a
[BODY] burning coal, but a glowing fire, burning or lightening. Compare
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><020923></sty>Exodus 9:23,25, with <sty c="800000" i="0"><197848></sty>Psalm 78:48; where it designates
[BODY] the fires or lightnings produced by thunder, which accompanied the hail. Lightning would be its mot proper rendering here; for instead of referring this verse to the plagues in Egypt, it may be considered as a continuation of what is contained in the foregoing verse; and the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Theodotion</sty> have rendered [<sty c="008080" i="0">rbd</sty>] in the preceding clause, not pestilence, but word — <sty c="0000d4" i="0">logov</sty>, its most usual meaning. This makes the whole to comport to what we read of God’s appearance on mount Sinai. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><021916></sty>Exodus 19:16; <sty c="800000" i="0"><053302></sty>Deuteronomy 33:2. The version then would be this —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">From before him proceeded the word (</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">i.e.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> the law;)</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And forth came lightning at his feet.</sty>

[BODY] Most of the ideas in this, and in the two preceding verses, seem to be similar to those we find in <sty c="800000" i="0"><053302></sty>Deuteronomy 33:2,3. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft50</sty> This verse is explained in a very striking manner, but the version is not
[BODY] so strictly correct It may be thus rendered: —

--- PAGE 176 ---

[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">6.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> He stood, and measure the earth;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">He looked, and agitated the earth;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And burst themselves open did the perpetual mountains,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Bend down did the hills of ages;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The going of ages were his.</sty>

[BODY] “The perpetual mountains” are literally “the mountains of perpetuity,” which had remained the same from the beginning. “The hills of ages” might be rendered the hills of antiquity or of old time, [<sty c="008080" i="0">µlw[</sty>], an indefinite past time. “The goings of ages,” are God’s proceedings, that is, in his works, and may therefore be rendered “deeds;” and they are said to be deeds “of ages,” <sty c="000000" i="1">i.e., </sty>of old time, with reference probably to the creation of the world: for he who makes perennial mountains to burst, and perpetual hills to bend downwards, must be their first creator. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft51</sty> The word [<sty c="008080" i="0">zwa</sty>] not only means iniquity, but also what iniquity
[BODY] produces, labor, trouble, affliction; and this latter meaning, as allowed by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, is most suitable to it here. The word is so taken in <sty c="800000" i="0"><013518></sty>Genesis 35:18; <sty c="800000" i="0"><052614></sty>Deuteronomy 26:14;
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><280904></sty>Hosea 9:4. Besides, this meaning makes a correspondence
[BODY] between this and the following line, as will be seen by the following version —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Under trouble have I seen the tents of Cushan,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Tremble did the curtains of the land of Madian.</sty>

[BODY] The “curtains” were those used in forming tents, and are used here to designate them. The most obvious reference here is to Cushan, mentioned in <sty c="800000" i="0"><070308></sty>Judges 3:8,10, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> states; yet some consider that it stands for <sty c="000000" i="1">Cush</sty>, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Lotan</sty>, in <sty c="800000" i="0"><012620></sty>Genesis 26:20, is put for <sty c="000000" i="1">Lot</sty>: and some, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Gesenius</sty>, say, that the African <sty c="000000" i="1">Cush</sty> is meant, and others, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, think, that it is the Arabian <sty c="000000" i="1">Cush,</sty> especially as <sty c="000000" i="1">Madian </sty>is also mentioned. Still the events recorded in Judges, nearly connected together, favor the opinion adopted by <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft52</sty> The two first lines present a difficulty in their construction. The most
[BODY] literal is this rendering of <sty c="000000" i="1">Junius</sty> —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Did against rivers kindle, O Jehovah —</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Against rivers, thy wrath;</sty>

[CENTERED] Our language will admit of a similar construction in another form, by inverting the order —

--- PAGE 177 ---

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Did thy wrath against rivers, O Jehovah,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Did it kindle against rivers?</sty>

[CENTERED] Some connect the two last lines of the verse with the previous one, thus —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Was thine indignation against the sea,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">When thou didst ride on thy horses,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">On </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">thy chariots of salvation?</sty>

[BODY] But <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> considers them rather as an answer to the previous questions, or as explanatory; and they may be thus rendered —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">When thou didst ride on thy horses,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Thy chariots were </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">those</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> of salvation.</sty>

[BODY] It is observed by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, that “there is no necessity for our understanding either the angels or thunder and lightning by ‘horses’ and ‘chariots.’ They are,” he adds, “merely figurative expressions, designed to carry out the metaphor adopted from military operations.” Or it may be, that the horses and chariots of the Israelites are here meant, as in the 11th verse, the arrows and spears of the people are spoken of as those of God. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft53</sty> This clause has been variously explained: the interpretation here given
[BODY] has been mostly adopted. In the Barberinean manuscript the whole of this prayer is given in many respects different from the present received text of the Septuagint, and this clause is thus found in it — <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ejcortasav bolidav thv faretrav ajutou</sty>. It is evident that this idea falls in more with the preceding clause than any other; and the Hebrew will admit of a sense bordering on this with less alteration than any other that has been offered. No version has been given without supposing somethin to be understood. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> says, that sixteen MSS. read [<sty c="008080" i="0">t[wbç</sty>]; by leaving out the [<sty c="008080" i="0">w</sty>], it may be a verb in Kal in the past tense, as rendered above, and writers might have easily put down [<sty c="008080" i="0">rma</sty>] for [<sty c="008080" i="0">rwza</sty>]. Then the line in Hebrew would be,
[CENTERED] [<sty c="008080" i="0">rwza twfm t[bç</sty>]
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“Thou hast filled with arrows the girdle.”</sty>

[BODY] It is a description of one equipped for battle; his bow was made ready, and he had filled his girdle, that is, his military guide, with arrows; for this girdle the preceding Greek version introduced the quiver, in which

--- PAGE 178 ---

[CENTERED] arrows were commonly carried. the word [<sty c="008080" i="0">twfm</sty>], means rods or staves, that is, of arrows, as we may take it here. This is the most satisfactory solution of the difficulties connected with this line, of which there have been, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> says, more than a hundred interpretation.
[BODY] The last clause of the verse is thus rendered by<sty c="000000" i="1"> Newcome</sty>, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Thou didst cleave the streams of the land;</sty>

[INDENT] and by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Thou didst cleave the earth into rivers.</sty>

[BODY] The words will not admit the first version; the genitive case in Hebrew is always by juxtaposition; here “streams” and “earth” are separated by the verb. The other version contains hardly a meaning. The most literal rendering is that given by <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>, and it afford the best sense. The words will admit of the following, which is materially the same, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">By streams didst thou cleavest the earth.</sty>

[BODY] The allusion evidently to the streams of that water which miraculously issued from the smitten rock, and followed the Israelites in the wilderness. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft54</sty> Most critics have overlooked the peculiar construction of this verse;
[BODY] but it presents a striking instant of the order in which the Prophets often arrange their ideas. There are two things referred to — the mountains and the waters — and the first verb regards both; the nominative case being anticipated, and the first of the two last lines refers to the waters, and the last to the mountains. This is the literal version, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">They saw thee, — in pain were the mountains,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The flood of waters passed away,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Utter did the deep its voice,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The height its hands lifted up.</sty>

[BODY] To construe [<sty c="008080" i="0">µwr</sty>] adverbially, “on high,” does not so well comport with the characters of the Hebrew language; and it evidently here refers to the “mountains,” as the “deep” refers to the water. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft55</sty> There is much beauty and force in this explanation: and accordant with
[BODY] it is the version of <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>. But that of <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> is somewhat different —

--- PAGE 179 ---

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">The sun</sty><sty c="000080" i="0"> and</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> the moon stood still </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">in their </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">habitation:</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">By </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">their </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">light thine arrows went abroad;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">By </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">their </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">brightness, the lightning of thy spear.</sty>

[BODY] To avoid the insertion of so many words in italics which are not in the original, I would render the verse thus —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">The sun! the moon! — it stood — she remained stationary,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For light to thine arrows </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">which </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">went forth,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For brightness to the flashing of thy spear.</sty>

[BODY] The genitive case is often to be rendered as a dative, as in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><243135></sty>Jeremiah 31:35, [<sty c="008080" i="0">hlyl rwal</sty>], “for the light of the night;” that
[BODY] is, “for light to the night.”
[BODY] There are twelve MSS. which have “and,” [<sty c="008080" i="0">w</sty>], before “moon:” but it is not wanted, the verb “stood” being singular; and it is followed, as I conceive, by another verb in the singular number, and in the feminine gender, while “stood” is in the masculine, and refers to the moon, and the last refers to the sun; which is sometimes feminine, while moon is ever the masculine. The verb [<sty c="008080" i="0">lbz</sty>] is not properly to dwell, but to continue fixed, or to remain stationary. The order in our language would be this —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">The sun remained stationary, the moon stood. — </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Ed.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft56</sty> However true is what is said here, it seems not to be the doctrine of
[BODY] this text. The version of <sty c="000000" i="1">Aquila</sty> and the <sty c="000000" i="1">Vulgate</sty> have been followed as to the second clause of the verse. The <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty> read, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">tou swsai ton</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">criston sou</sty> — to save thy Christ;” or, according to Alex. cod., “thy Christs — <sty c="0000d4" i="0">touv cristouv sou</sty>;” or, according to Barb. MS., “thine elect — <sty c="0000d4" i="0">touv eklektouv sou</sty>.” Five Hebrew MSS. have [<sty c="008080" i="0">dyjyçm</sty>], “thine anointed ones.” But if “people” in the preceding line; or it may refer to Joshua and his successors, the singular being used, as it is often done by the Prophets, in the collective sense. The particle [<sty c="008080" i="0">ta</sty>] before it is not often used as a preposition; and the word [<sty c="008080" i="0">[çy</sty>] may better be taken here as a verb, according to the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>, than as a noun, though as a verb it most commonly occurs in <sty c="000000" i="1">Hiphil</sty>: but see <sty c="800000" i="0"><092305></sty>1 Samuel 23:5; <sty c="800000" i="0"><100806></sty>2 Samuel 8:6. The following would then be the version —

--- PAGE 180 ---

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Go forth didst thou to save thy people,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">To save thine anointed:</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Thou didst smite the head from the house of the wicked,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Emptying out the foundation even to the neck.</sty>

[BODY] The reference in the two lines is evidently to the rooting out of the Canaanites, and not, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> thinks, to the destruction of the firstborn in Egypt. The singular is poetically used for the plural: “head,” instead of heads, or chiefs, etc. The last line seems to be a proverbial saying, signifying an entire demolition, the very foundation being dug up, though so deep as to reach up to man’s neck. There is no MSS. nor version to countenance [<sty c="008080" i="0">rwx</sty>], “rock,” which <sty c="000000" i="1">Houbigant</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>adopt. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft57</sty> The Keri and many MSS. read [<sty c="008080" i="0">wyzrp</sty>], “his villages;” but there is no
[BODY] need of this change, for the singular is used throughout instead of the plural, until we come to the two following lines; and this proves that the singular is to be taken in a collective sense. <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> renders it “captains,” contrary to the meaning of the word in other parts. It means an open unfortified village, as it were scattered, and without any boundaries. <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft58</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>and some others, without any authority, read “thy rod;” but
[BODY] conjecture, without some solid reason, cannot be allowed. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft59</sty> “To devour the poor in secret,” seems to have an allusion to the
[BODY] practice of wild beasts, who take there prey to their dens to devour it there. The poor her, as in many other places, mean the helpless, such as are destitute of aid or power to resist their enemies. The line may be thus rendered —
[BODY] Their joy <sty c="000000" i="1">was,</sty> as it were, to devour the helpless in secret. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft60</sty> The word is [<sty c="008080" i="0">rmj</sty>], which many have rendered <sty c="000000" i="1">acervus</sty> — heap; but
[BODY] there is no clear instance in which it has such a meaning. It is without a preposition, and the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint </sty>render it by a participle, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">tarassontav</sty>, which agrees with “horses.” It is singular in Hebrew, and, if a particple, it agrees with the nominative case to the preceding verb, [<sty c="008080" i="0">tkrd</sty>], “thou didst guide” or direct. The two lines might then be rendered thus, —
[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">Thou didst guide through the sea thy horses,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">Disturbing mighty waters.</sty>

--- PAGE 181 ---

[BODY] Both <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> think that the passage through the Red Sea is not what is meant; but the subjugation of the Canaanites, conveyed in a language derived from that event. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft61</sty> The word [<sty c="008080" i="0">rça</sty>], which <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> renders <sty c="000000" i="1">ut</sty>, “that,” has occasioned great
[BODY] trouble to critics. <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty> reads <sty c="000000" i="1">qui</sty> — “who,” “Who shall rest,” etc.; <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, “yet,” “Yet I shall have rest,” etc. But it is never found as an adversative. The construction of this line and the following is very difficult; and many have been the forms in which they have been rendered. The verb [<sty c="008080" i="0">jwn</sty>] means not only to <sty c="000000" i="1">rest</sty> from action or labor, but also to <sty c="000000" i="1">rest </sty>in the sense of remaining or continuing. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><120215></sty>2 Kings 2:15, and <sty c="800000" i="0"><230202></sty>Isaiah 2:2. And were it taken in this latter sense here, there would be a consistency in the whole passage. The Prophet describes first the dread which seized him on hearing the report of God’s vengeance; and then in the two last lines he accounts for his consternation, because he should remain to witness this vengeance; and he proceeds in verse 17 to set forth the effects of it, and in verse 18 he states that he would still rejoice in the God of his salvation. The three verses may be thus rendered, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">16.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> I heard, — and tremble did my bowels;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">At the voice my lips quivered;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Enter does rottenness into my bones,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And on my own account I tremble;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Because I shall remain to the day of distress,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">To his coming up to the people, who shall invade me.</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">17.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> For the fig-tree shall not shoot forth,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And no produce shall be on the vines;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Fail shall the fruit of the olive,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And the fields, none shall yield food;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Cut off from the fold shall be the sheep,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And no ox shall be in the stalls:</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">18.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> But as for me, in Jehovah will I rejoice,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">I will exult in the God of my salvation.</sty>

[BODY] “On my own account,” or for myself, [<sty c="008080" i="0">ytjt</sty>]: the preposition, [<sty c="008080" i="0">tjt</sty>], is often taken in this sense; See <sty c="800000" i="0"><101921></sty>2 Samuel 19:21, <sty c="800000" i="0"><203021></sty>Proverbs 30:21. “Invade us” or assault us, or them, the people, [<sty c="008080" i="0">wndwgy</sty>]; for [<sty c="008080" i="0">wn</sty>] is either us or him, but in our language them, for so we speak of people. “And the fields, none,” etc. There are instances of [<sty c="008080" i="0">al</sty>], as here, in which it may be rendered “none” and “nothing.” See

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft63</sty> No satisfactory conjectures have been made by any as to the<sty c="000000" i="1"> my </sty>added
[BODY] to this word. Hezekiah says at the end of his prayer, <sty c="800000" i="0"><233820></sty>Isaiah 38:20, [<sty c="008080" i="0">ytwnygnw zgnn</sty>], “and my neginoth will we sing,” or play, etc. Our version makes this <sty c="000000" i="1">my </sty>to refer to the ode or song he made to be played on the neginoth, supposed to have been a stringed instrument. In this case, “my neginoth” means the song he made for the neginoth. Then we might render the words, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">For the leader; my song on the stringed instruments. — </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Ed.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft64</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> makes here a change in the discourse; but the whole to the end
[BODY] of the chapter may be viewed as the parable or the taunt mentioned in verse 6, and the particle Ho! may be retained instead of Wo. The taunt seems to have been formed so as to have been especially suitable to be used by the Jews.
[BODY] By regarding the passage in this light, we can understand the sudden change off person in verse 16, if the proposed emendation be disapproved; for we see the same in the former portions of the “taunt.” See 6 and 7, and also 9 and 10. That the reader may see the whole of this passage, containing the “taunt,” in the light in which I am now fully inclined to regard it, it shall be presented to him complete: — <sty c="800000" i="0">6.</sty> Will not these, every one of them, Raise up a proverb concerning him, And a taunt, enigmas for him, and say, — “Ho! He increases what is not his! how long! And he accumulates on himself thick clay! —

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">7.</sty> Will they not suddenly rise up — thy biters, And awake — thy tormentors, And thou become booties to them?

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">8.</sty> For thou hast spoiled many nation, And spoil thee shall all the remnant of the people, On account of men’s blood, and of violence To the land, to the city, and to all its inhabitants.”

--- PAGE 183 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">9.</sty> “Ho! he has coveted an evil covetousness to his house, To set on high his nest, In order to save himself from the hand of evil! —

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">10.</sty> Thou hast consulted shame to thine house, By cutting off many nations And by sinning against thine own soul:

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">11.</sty> For the stone — from the wall it cries, And the beam — from the woodwork it answers it, —

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">12.</sty> ‘Ho! he builds a town by blood, And sets up a city by oppression!’ —

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">13.</sty> Shall nothing <sty c="000000" i="1">be</sty>, lo! From Jehovah of hosts? Yea, labor shall the people for the fire, And nations — for vanity shall they weary themselves:

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">14.</sty> For filled shall be the earth With the knowledge of the glory of Jehovah, Like the waters which spread over the sea.”

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">15.</sty> “Ho! he gives drink to his neighbour! — Thou addest thy bottle and also strong drink, In order to look on their nakedness!

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">16.</sty> Thou hast been filled with reproach rather than with glory: Drink thou also, and be uncovered; Come round to thee shall the cup Of the right hand of Jehovah; And shameful spewing shall be on thy glory:

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">17.</sty> For the violence done to Lebanon — it shall overwhelm thee, And the plunder of beasts — it shall rend thee; On account of men’s blood, and of violence To the land, to the city, and to all its inhabitants.”

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">18.</sty> “What avails the graven image! For its graver — he forms it — Even the molten image and the teacher of falsehood: Yea, trust in it does the former of its form, After having made dumb idols!

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">19.</sty> Ho! he saith to the wood, ‘Arise, Awake;’ To the dumb stone, ‘It will teach?’ Behold it! covered it is with gold and silver, Yet there is no spirit within it! But Jehovah is in his holy temple: Silent at his presence let the whole earth be.”

--- PAGE 184 ---

[BODY] The “taunt” <sty c="000000" i="1">may</sty> be deemed as terminating at the end of the 17th verse; but I regard it as continuing to the end of the chapter. The word “neighbour,” in the 15th verse, is a collective singular, meaning every neighbour: hence “their” at the end of the verse. The same may be said of “image” in verse 18, which means every image or images, as “idols” are mentioned afterwards. Such are common instances in the Prophets. “It will teach,” in verse 19, most evidently refers to “the dumb stone” — the idol; for it is expressly called “the teacher of falsehood” in verse 17. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed</sty>
